[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar II Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I have stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going, like salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking for the neighbors, or even walking into my shop just to enjoy the smell of the wood.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is… I’ve stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I don’t mean just being a little bored or needing a change of pace. It’s more like the spark’s gone out of things that used to feel like me.

I used to go to salsa nights, and I loved that — the music, seeing familiar faces, just moving around and not thinking too much. Church gatherings too, I’d usually show up and talk with people, help where I could. I liked cooking for the neighbors, making enough so somebody could take a plate home. And even my shop… I’m a carpenter, and sometimes I’d walk in there just to be around it, you know? The smell of the wood, the tools, the quiet of it — that used to settle me.

Now I don’t even want to do those things. I think about going, or cooking, or stepping into the shop, and it just feels like there’s nothing in me reaching for it. That’s what made me think something’s really wrong.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really. I mean, that’s the thing that scares me a little. Even if I make myself show up, it doesn’t seem to land the same way.

Like with salsa nights — I used to feel the music before I even got there, if that makes sense. I’d be thinking about the rhythm, who might be there, just the whole feeling of it. Now I can picture myself going and it feels flat, like I’m just moving a body through a room. Same with church gatherings. I used to get something from being around people there, talking, helping out, feeling connected. Lately even the thought of it feels like work, and if I do anything like that, I don’t come away feeling lifted.

Cooking for the neighbors used to be one of those simple good things for me. I liked making extra, packing up a plate, knowing someone would enjoy it. Now I’ll think about it and there’s just… nothing pulling me toward it. And my shop, that one bothers me most maybe. I’m a carpenter, so that place has always been more than just where I work. I used to walk in just to smell the wood, touch the tools, stand there in the quiet. It used to settle me. Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.

And I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.

A lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.

I keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.

I liked making extra, packing up a plate, knowing someone would enjoy it. Now I’ll think about it and there’s just… nothing pulling me toward it. And my shop, that one bothers me most maybe. I’m a carpenter, so that place has always been more than just where I work. I used to walk in just to smell the wood, touch the tools, stand there in the quiet. It used to settle me. Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”

But it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.

It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.

But this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.

And I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.

I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.

It’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.

I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.

There are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.

But then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.

It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.

Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.

I’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.

Other times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I don’t mean just being a little bored or needing a change of pace. It’s more like the spark’s gone out of things that used to feel like me.\n\nI used to go to salsa nights, and I loved that — the music, seeing familiar faces, just moving around and not thinking too much. Church gatherings too, I’d usually show up and talk with people, help where I could. I liked cooking for the neighbors, making enough so somebody could take a plate home. And even my shop… I’m a carpenter, and sometimes I’d walk in there just to be around it, you know? The smell of the wood, the tools, the quiet of it — that used to settle me.\n\nNow I don’t even want to do those things. I think about going, or cooking, or stepping into the shop, and it just feels like there’s nothing in me reaching for it. That’s what made me think something’s really wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, that’s the thing that scares me a little. Even if I make myself show up, it doesn’t seem to land the same way.\n\nLike with salsa nights — I used to feel the music before I even got there, if that makes sense. I’d be thinking about the rhythm, who might be there, just the whole feeling of it. Now I can picture myself going and it feels flat, like I’m just moving a body through a room. Same with church gatherings. I used to get something from being around people there, talking, helping out, feeling connected. Lately even the thought of it feels like work, and if I do anything like that, I don’t come away feeling lifted.\n\nCooking for the neighbors used to be one of those simple good things for me. I liked making extra, packing up a plate, knowing someone would enjoy it. Now I’ll think about it and there’s just… nothing pulling me toward it. And my shop, that one bothers me most maybe. I’m a carpenter, so that place has always been more than just where I work. I used to walk in just to smell the wood, touch the tools, stand there in the quiet. It used to settle me. Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. and handle my wife’s care and every job at the same time without dropping a thing.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: When I was in that keyed-up state, every saw noise, phone buzz, or bit of movement in the shop pulled me off track, and I would jump from one half-done task to another.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I took on extra restoration jobs, started mentoring the younger carpenters harder than usual, drew up business plans, and reached out to people I normally would have waited weeks to call.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I have had to hand off parts of jobs I normally supervise myself, and I have pulled away from church, friends, and neighbors to the point that my wife and parish contact got worried about my safety.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in my mood and behavior has been going on for about two months, since my wife’s health suddenly declined and our routine changed.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The depression has been dragging on for about six months now, long enough that it no longer feels like just a bad week or ordinary stress.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.\n\nI’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.\n\nOther times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any periods of feeling somewhat better over the past six months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… maybe there are little moments where I can get through an hour or an afternoon a bit easier, but I wouldn’t call it a real stretch of feeling better.

This has been dragging on for about six months now, and that’s part of what scares me. At first I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or work stress, or I was tired and needed to push through it. But it’s been long enough now that it doesn’t feel like that anymore. It feels like this thing that just settled in and stayed.

So, no… I haven’t had any real period where I thought, “Okay, I’m coming out of this.” It’s more like I’ve had small breaks in the day sometimes, but the overall thing has still been there for months.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I don’t mean just being a little bored or needing a change of pace. It’s more like the spark’s gone out of things that used to feel like me.\n\nI used to go to salsa nights, and I loved that — the music, seeing familiar faces, just moving around and not thinking too much. Church gatherings too, I’d usually show up and talk with people, help where I could. I liked cooking for the neighbors, making enough so somebody could take a plate home. And even my shop… I’m a carpenter, and sometimes I’d walk in there just to be around it, you know? The smell of the wood, the tools, the quiet of it — that used to settle me.\n\nNow I don’t even want to do those things. I think about going, or cooking, or stepping into the shop, and it just feels like there’s nothing in me reaching for it. That’s what made me think something’s really wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, that’s the thing that scares me a little. Even if I make myself show up, it doesn’t seem to land the same way.\n\nLike with salsa nights — I used to feel the music before I even got there, if that makes sense. I’d be thinking about the rhythm, who might be there, just the whole feeling of it. Now I can picture myself going and it feels flat, like I’m just moving a body through a room. Same with church gatherings. I used to get something from being around people there, talking, helping out, feeling connected. Lately even the thought of it feels like work, and if I do anything like that, I don’t come away feeling lifted.\n\nCooking for the neighbors used to be one of those simple good things for me. I liked making extra, packing up a plate, knowing someone would enjoy it. Now I’ll think about it and there’s just… nothing pulling me toward it. And my shop, that one bothers me most maybe. I’m a carpenter, so that place has always been more than just where I work. I used to walk in just to smell the wood, touch the tools, stand there in the quiet. It used to settle me. Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.\n\nI’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.\n\nOther times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling somewhat better over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe there are little moments where I can get through an hour or an afternoon a bit easier, but I wouldn’t call it a real stretch of feeling better.\n\nThis has been dragging on for about six months now, and that’s part of what scares me. At first I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or work stress, or I was tired and needed to push through it. But it’s been long enough now that it doesn’t feel like that anymore. It feels like this thing that just settled in and stayed.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t had any real period where I thought, “Okay, I’m coming out of this.” It’s more like I’ve had small breaks in the day sometimes, but the overall thing has still been there for months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah. That’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it hits me right where I’ve always been pretty solid.

I’ll be looking at blueprints — plans I should be able to read without even thinking, I’ve been doing this work my whole adult life — and I’ll just stare at them. And then I’ll have to ask one of the younger guys to repeat something they just told me, or go over a measurement again, and that’s embarrassing. Not because they’re doing anything wrong, but because I’m the one who should be catching it right away. I should be the guy they ask, not the guy standing there saying, “Wait, say that again?” It makes me feel… I don’t know, exposed. Like I’m not as sharp as I’m supposed to be.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I don’t mean just being a little bored or needing a change of pace. It’s more like the spark’s gone out of things that used to feel like me.\n\nI used to go to salsa nights, and I loved that — the music, seeing familiar faces, just moving around and not thinking too much. Church gatherings too, I’d usually show up and talk with people, help where I could. I liked cooking for the neighbors, making enough so somebody could take a plate home. And even my shop… I’m a carpenter, and sometimes I’d walk in there just to be around it, you know? The smell of the wood, the tools, the quiet of it — that used to settle me.\n\nNow I don’t even want to do those things. I think about going, or cooking, or stepping into the shop, and it just feels like there’s nothing in me reaching for it. That’s what made me think something’s really wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you're doing, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, that’s the thing that scares me a little. Even if I make myself show up, it doesn’t seem to land the same way.\n\nLike with salsa nights — I used to feel the music before I even got there, if that makes sense. I’d be thinking about the rhythm, who might be there, just the whole feeling of it. Now I can picture myself going and it feels flat, like I’m just moving a body through a room. Same with church gatherings. I used to get something from being around people there, talking, helping out, feeling connected. Lately even the thought of it feels like work, and if I do anything like that, I don’t come away feeling lifted.\n\nCooking for the neighbors used to be one of those simple good things for me. I liked making extra, packing up a plate, knowing someone would enjoy it. Now I’ll think about it and there’s just… nothing pulling me toward it. And my shop, that one bothers me most maybe. I’m a carpenter, so that place has always been more than just where I work. I used to walk in just to smell the wood, touch the tools, stand there in the quiet. It used to settle me. Now I can walk in there and it’s like I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most of the time, especially in the mornings. I’ll wake up and before I’ve even really thought about anything, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the whole house has lost its color somehow. Everything looks the same, my wife is there, the furniture is there, the day is starting like it always does, but inside it all feels dim.\n\nAnd I try not to show it right away. I really do. I’ll lie there for a bit and tell myself, “Get up, don’t make a scene, don’t worry her,” because I don’t want my wife seeing me break down first thing in the morning. So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.\n\nI’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.\n\nOther times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling somewhat better over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe there are little moments where I can get through an hour or an afternoon a bit easier, but I wouldn’t call it a real stretch of feeling better.\n\nThis has been dragging on for about six months now, and that’s part of what scares me. At first I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or work stress, or I was tired and needed to push through it. But it’s been long enough now that it doesn’t feel like that anymore. It feels like this thing that just settled in and stayed.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t had any real period where I thought, “Okay, I’m coming out of this.” It’s more like I’ve had small breaks in the day sometimes, but the overall thing has still been there for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it hits me right where I’ve always been pretty solid.\n\nI’ll be looking at blueprints — plans I should be able to read without even thinking, I’ve been doing this work my whole adult life — and I’ll just stare at them. The lines are there, the measurements are there, I know I’ve read stuff like that a thousand times, but it’s like my head won’t organize it. The lines kind of blur together in my mind, not my eyes exactly, more like I can’t make sense of them fast enough.\n\nAnd then I’ll have to ask one of the younger guys to repeat something they just told me, or go over a measurement again, and that’s embarrassing. Not because they’re doing anything wrong, but because I’m the one who should be catching it right away. I should be the guy they ask, not the guy standing there saying, “Wait, say that again?” It makes me feel… I don’t know, exposed. Like I’m not as sharp as I’m supposed to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
