So I kind of brace myself before I even get out of bed. Sometimes I’m just forcing my face to stay normal, forcing my voice to sound normal. But underneath it, yes, I feel sad… or empty, maybe. Heavy. Like I’m carrying something before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I keep going back over everything in my head — every appointment, every little change in her health, things she said, things I noticed or didn’t notice. I’ll be lying there or sitting alone and suddenly I’m replaying it like I’m studying a piece of work I messed up, trying to find the exact moment where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI keep thinking, you know, a better husband would have caught it sooner. A better husband would have pushed harder, asked the right question, noticed the right sign, protected her somehow. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair to themselves. But when it’s me, it feels true. It feels like I failed at the one job that mattered most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About six months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to pin down the exact day it started, because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, or that I was tired, or that life had just been heavy lately. You know, one bad week becomes two, then a month, and you keep thinking, “I’ll shake out of it once things settle.”\n\nBut it hasn’t really lifted. It’s been dragging on long enough now that I can’t honestly call it ordinary stress anymore. Six months is a long time to wake up feeling like this and keep hoping tomorrow will feel different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you can perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of the things that’s scared me a little, because I’ve always been someone who could push through physical work. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Not because I don’t know what to do, but because everything takes more effort than it should. I’ll start a chore and have to stop, or I’ll move around like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s frustrating, because in my head I still think of myself as the guy who gets things done, but lately my body just doesn’t seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I don’t weigh myself much unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something, and even then I don’t always remember the number.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think — like I haven’t suddenly had to punch new holes in my belt or anything obvious like that. But I also haven’t been paying close attention. If there’s been a change, it’s not one that jumped out at me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there all day with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had thoughts where I think… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it, at least I wouldn’t have to keep trying to drag myself through another day feeling this way.\n\nAnd then sometimes — and this is the part that really scares me — I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I don’t want to believe that, and part of me knows they’d be hurt, but when I’m really low it starts feeling like I’m just a burden or like I’m failing everybody by being like this.\n\nI’m ashamed of it, honestly. I haven’t wanted to tell anyone because once you say it, it feels… serious. But yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess, which sounds strange, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nThere are times at work where I’m just moving painfully slow. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. I know exactly what needs doing — it’s not confusion — but my body just won’t get in gear. And it’s slow enough that people have noticed. Guys at the job have made comments, not always mean, but like, “You all right?” or “You’re taking forever with that,” and that gets under my skin because I know they can see it.\n\nBut then other times I’m not slow so much as… restless and useless. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.\n\nI’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.\n\nOther times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling somewhat better over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe there are little moments where I can get through an hour or an afternoon a bit easier, but I wouldn’t call it a real stretch of feeling better.\n\nThis has been dragging on for about six months now, and that’s part of what scares me. At first I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or work stress, or I was tired and needed to push through it. But it’s been long enough now that it doesn’t feel like that anymore. It feels like this thing that just settled in and stayed.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t had any real period where I thought, “Okay, I’m coming out of this.” It’s more like I’ve had small breaks in the day sometimes, but the overall thing has still been there for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it hits me right where I’ve always been pretty solid.\n\nI’ll be looking at blueprints — plans I should be able to read without even thinking, I’ve been doing this work my whole adult life — and I’ll just stare at them. The lines are there, the measurements are there, I know I’ve read stuff like that a thousand times, but it’s like my head won’t organize it. The lines kind of blur together in my mind, not my eyes exactly, more like I can’t make sense of them fast enough.\n\nAnd then I’ll have to ask one of the younger guys to repeat something they just told me, or go over a measurement again, and that’s embarrassing. Not because they’re doing anything wrong, but because I’m the one who should be catching it right away. I should be the guy they ask, not the guy standing there saying, “Wait, say that again?” It makes me feel… I don’t know, exposed. Like I’m not as sharp as I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any times when you felt like you were moving slower than usual or had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, honestly. And it’s strange because they’re almost opposite things, but people at the job have noticed both.\n\nThere are days where I move like my boots are full of wet cement. That’s the best way I can describe it. Something simple, like picking up a board or carrying it across the shop, takes me forever. I’ll know exactly what I’m supposed to be doing, but my body just feels slow, like there’s this delay between deciding to move and actually moving. And it’s not me being lazy. I know what lazy feels like. This is different. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should, even the basic stuff I’ve done for years without thinking.\n\nThen other times I can’t settle at all. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to where the tools are, then turn around, then look at the workbench, then go back again. But I’m not actually getting anything useful done. It’s just motion. Like I’m circling the work instead of doing it. And that bothers me because in a shop, movement is supposed to have a purpose. You grab a tool, measure, cut, carry, clamp — whatever it is. But I’ll be moving around and still not accomplishing anything.\n\nAnd the worst part is when I can tell the guys are noticing. Nobody has to say much. You can feel it. Like they’re wondering why I’m taking so long with something simple, or why I’m wandering around rubbing my hands like I lost track of what I was doing. That makes me feel embarrassed, and then I get even more stuck in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current restlessness and pacing are more prominent than your feelings of being slowed down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say one is more prominent all the time. It kind of switches on me, and that’s part of what makes it feel so strange.\n\nPeople at work have noticed both, I think. Some days I’m slowed way down, like I said — like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board or move something across the shop, and it takes me forever, even though it’s something I’ve done a million times. I can feel myself dragging.\n\nBut then other times it’s the pacing that stands out more. I’ll be walking around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one spot to another, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. It’s not productive movement. It’s just… restless, stuck movement.\n\nSo if you’re asking which is worse, I’m not sure I can separate them cleanly. The pacing is more noticeable maybe because I’m moving around, but the slowed-down feeling is just as real. Both have been obvious enough that the guys at the job can tell something’s off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: More than I’m comfortable admitting, honestly.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it comes up… sometimes when I’m really low or when I feel like I’m just dragging everybody down. I’ll catch myself thinking, “Maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me like this.” And then as soon as I think it, it scares me, because I don’t want to be thinking that way about my own family.\n\nSometimes it’s more like… not exactly a plan or anything, but this thought of, “If I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.” Easier for them, easier for everyone. And I hate saying that out loud. It makes me feel ashamed, because I love my family, and I know that’s a terrible thing to put into words. But yeah, those thoughts do come. Sometimes. More than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way or anything.\n\nI don’t think there’s been some huge obvious change where I’d say, “Yeah, I stopped eating,” or “I’m eating constantly.” At least not that I’ve clearly noticed. Some days I eat more normally, some days I just sort of pick at whatever’s around, but I don’t know if that’s an appetite change or just me not paying much attention.\n\nMy wife might notice that kind of thing better than I do, honestly. I’m not great at clocking it unless it’s really dramatic. Over the past six months, I can’t point to anything definite with my appetite.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least not a significant amount that I’m aware of.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that, and nobody’s said, “Hey, you’ve lost a bunch of weight.” I haven’t really been getting on a scale regularly, so I can’t give you a number. Maybe it’s gone up or down a little here and there, like anyone’s does, but over the past six months I can’t point to some clear weight loss.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… actually, both, which is part of what makes it feel so strange.

There are times at work where I feel like I’m moving through mud. Like my boots are full of wet cement or something. I’ll go to pick up a board or move something across the shop, and it’s like everything takes twice as long as it should. Not because I don’t know what I’m doing — I’ve been doing carpentry long enough that my body usually just knows the motions — but lately I’ll just be slow. Heavy. Like my arms and legs don’t quite answer right away.

And then other times it flips the other direction. I’ll catch myself pacing around the shop, rubbing my hands, walking from one bench to another like I’m about to do something, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Just sort of circling, getting in my own way. People at the job have noticed it too, which is embarrassing. A guy’ll say, “You alright?” or “You looking for something?” and I don’t even know how to answer because I’m not looking for anything, I’m just… stuck moving but not getting anywhere.

So I guess if you’re asking whether I’m restless or slowed down, it’s not cleanly one or the other. It’s like I either can barely get myself going, or I can’t settle enough to do the thing in front of me.

More than I probably should, but it doesn’t feel like I can just tell myself to stop.\n\nA lot of it is about my wife. I’m a carpenter, so I know what tired feels like after a real day’s work — like after framing a roof all day, hauling lumber, being up and down ladders, that kind of worn-out where at least it makes sense.\n\nBut this is different. I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. I haven’t been keeping close track of it. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to one bench, then back to another, and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s like I can’t settle into the task, but I’m not making progress either. Just burning nervous energy and looking busy without doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve always taken pride in being steady and efficient, and now I either feel stuck in mud or like I’m circling the work instead of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you're feeling restless, is it more like you can't sit still or you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: It’s more pacing, I think… but it’s not just pacing like I’m trying to get exercise or blow off steam. It’s this useless kind of movement.\n\nI’ll be in the shop and I’ll walk over to one bench like I’m about to start something, then I’ll stop and rub my hands together, then turn around and go back toward where I just was. Then I’ll look at a board or a tool like I know what needs to happen, but I don’t actually do it. I just keep circling around the work. And I can feel people noticing, which makes it worse, because then I’m restless and self-conscious at the same time.\n\nOther times it’s the opposite, like I said — I’m not pacing, I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Picking up a board or carrying something across the shop takes me forever, and guys at work have noticed that too. So it kind of flips between me being stuck in slow motion and me pacing around rubbing my hands, not actually getting anything useful done.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling somewhat better over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. At first I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or work stress, or I was tired and needed to push through it. But it’s been long enough now that it doesn’t feel like that anymore. It feels like this thing that just settled in and stayed.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t had any real period where I thought, “Okay, I’m coming out of this.” It’s more like I’ve had small breaks in the day sometimes, but the overall thing has still been there for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it hits me right where I’ve always been pretty solid.\n\nI’ll be looking at blueprints — plans I should be able to read without even thinking, I’ve been doing this work my whole adult life — and I’ll just stare at them. The lines are there, the measurements are there, I know I’ve read stuff like that a thousand times, but it’s like my head won’t organize it. The lines kind of blur together in my mind, not my eyes exactly, more like I can’t make sense of them fast enough.\n\nAnd then I’ll have to ask one of the younger guys to repeat something they just told me, or go over a measurement again, and that’s embarrassing. Not because they’re doing anything wrong, but because I’m the one who should be catching it right away. I should be the guy they ask, not the guy standing there saying, “Wait, say that again?” It makes me feel… I don’t know, exposed. Like I’m not as sharp as I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any times when you felt like you were moving slower than usual or had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, honestly. And it’s strange because they’re almost opposite things, but people at the job have noticed both.\n\nThere are days where I move like my boots are full of wet cement. That’s the best way I can describe it. Something simple, like picking up a board or carrying it across the shop, takes me forever. I’ll know exactly what I’m supposed to be doing, but my body just feels slow, like there’s this delay between deciding to move and actually moving. And it’s not me being lazy. I know what lazy feels like. This is different. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should, even the basic stuff I’ve done for years without thinking.\n\nThen other times I can’t settle at all. I’ll pace around the shop, rub my hands together, walk over to where the tools are, then turn around, then look at the workbench, then go back again. But I’m not actually getting anything useful done. It’s just motion. Like I’m circling the work instead of doing it. And that bothers me because in a shop, movement is supposed to have a purpose. You grab a tool, measure, cut, carry, clamp — whatever it is. But I’ll be moving around and still not accomplishing anything.\n\nAnd the worst part is when I can tell the guys are noticing. Nobody has to say much. You can feel it. Like they’re wondering why I’m taking so long with something simple, or why I’m wandering around rubbing my hands like I lost track of what I was doing. That makes me feel embarrassed, and then I get even more stuck in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current restlessness and pacing are more prominent than your feelings of being slowed down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say one is more prominent all the time. It kind of switches on me, and that’s part of what makes it feel so strange.\n\nPeople at work have noticed both, I think. Some days I’m slowed way down, like I said — like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board or move something across the shop, and it takes me forever, even though it’s something I’ve done a million times. I can feel myself dragging.\n\nBut then other times it’s the pacing that stands out more. I’ll be walking around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one spot to another, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. It’s not productive movement. It’s just… restless, stuck movement.\n\nSo if you’re asking which is worse, I’m not sure I can separate them cleanly. The pacing is more noticeable maybe because I’m moving around, but the slowed-down feeling is just as real. Both have been obvious enough that the guys at the job can tell something’s off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: More than I’m comfortable admitting, honestly.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it comes up… sometimes when I’m really low or when I feel like I’m just dragging everybody down. I’ll catch myself thinking, “Maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me like this.” And then as soon as I think it, it scares me, because I don’t want to be thinking that way about my own family.\n\nSometimes it’s more like… not exactly a plan or anything, but this thought of, “If I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.” Easier for them, easier for everyone. And I hate saying that out loud. It makes me feel ashamed, because I love my family, and I know that’s a terrible thing to put into words. But yeah, those thoughts do come. Sometimes. More than I wish they did.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way or anything.\n\nI don’t think there’s been some huge obvious change where I’d say, “Yeah, I stopped eating,” or “I’m eating constantly.” At least not that I’ve clearly noticed. Some days I eat more normally, some days I just sort of pick at whatever’s around, but I don’t know if that’s an appetite change or just me not paying much attention.\n\nMy wife might notice that kind of thing better than I do, honestly. I’m not great at clocking it unless it’s really dramatic. Over the past six months, I can’t point to anything definite with my appetite.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least not a significant amount that I’m aware of.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that, and nobody’s said, “Hey, you’ve lost a bunch of weight.” I haven’t really been getting on a scale regularly, so I can’t give you a number. Maybe it’s gone up or down a little here and there, like anyone’s does, but over the past six months I can’t point to some clear weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're currently experiencing more restlessness or feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, both, which is part of what makes it feel so strange.\n\nThere are times at work where I feel like I’m moving through mud. Like my boots are full of wet cement or something. I’ll go to pick up a board or move something across the shop, and it’s like everything takes twice as long as it should. Not because I don’t know what I’m doing — I’ve been doing carpentry long enough that my body usually just knows the motions — but lately I’ll just be slow. Heavy. Like my arms and legs don’t quite answer right away.\n\nAnd then other times it flips the other direction. I’ll catch myself pacing around the shop, rubbing my hands, walking from one bench to another like I’m about to do something, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Just sort of circling, getting in my own way. People at the job have noticed it too, which is embarrassing. A guy’ll say, “You alright?” or “You looking for something?” and I don’t even know how to answer because I’m not looking for anything, I’m just… stuck moving but not getting anywhere.\n\nSo I guess if you’re asking whether I’m restless or slowed down, it’s not cleanly one or the other. It’s like I either can barely get myself going, or I can’t settle enough to do the thing in front of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah, like I was saying… it’s kind of both, and people at work have noticed it.

Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll be in the shop trying to pick up a board or carry something across the room, and it feels like it takes me forever to do something that should be automatic. I know what I’m doing, but my body just feels slow and heavy.

And then other times I’m the opposite. I’ll get stuck pacing around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m about to do something, but I’m not actually getting anything useful done. It’s not like productive energy. It’s more like I can’t settle, but I’m also not accomplishing anything.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because the guys can see it. I’m not just imagining it in my head. They’ll kind of look at me like, “What are you doing?” and I don’t really have a good answer.

I can get a full night in bed, or I can sit down for a while thinking I just need to rest, and when I stand up my body still feels used up. Like there’s no reserve. Even small things feel too big. Loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times without thinking, can feel like I’m staring at a whole mountain of work. I’ll look at the toolbox or the saws and just think, “How am I supposed to do all that?” when it’s not even that much.\n\nAnd I’m slower, too. Like my arms and legs don’t quite answer right away.\n\nAnd then other times it flips the other direction. I’ll catch myself pacing around the shop, rubbing my hands, walking from one bench to another like I’m about to do something, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Just sort of circling, getting in my own way. People at the job have noticed it too, which is embarrassing. A guy’ll say, “You alright?” or “You looking for something?” and I don’t even know how to answer because I’m not looking for anything, I’m just… stuck moving but not getting anywhere.\n\nSo I guess if you’re asking whether I’m restless or slowed down, it’s not cleanly one or the other. It’s like I either can barely get myself going, or I can’t settle enough to do the thing in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing more restlessness or feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was saying… it’s kind of both, and people at work have noticed it.\n\nSome days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll be in the shop trying to pick up a board or carry something across the room, and it feels like it takes me forever to do something that should be automatic. I know what I’m doing, but my body just feels slow and heavy.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite. I’ll get stuck pacing around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m about to do something, but I’m not actually getting anything useful done. It’s not like productive energy. It’s more like I can’t settle, but I’m also not accomplishing anything.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because the guys can see it. I’m not just imagining it in my head. They’ll kind of look at me like, “What are you doing?” and I don’t really have a good answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I’ll go to pick up a board or carry something across the shop, and it feels like my boots are full of wet cement. It’s like I either can barely get myself going, or I can’t settle enough to do the thing in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing more restlessness or feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was saying… it’s kind of both, and people at work have noticed it.\n\nSome days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I know what I’m doing, but my body just feels slow and heavy.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite. It’s been pretty continuous, yeah.
